across CFI, RMSEA, SRMR, and the Bollen –Stine bootstrap χ2tests
and as such are both presented in full in Table 4 . When comparing the
models with best ﬁt indices, Model 5 provided a signi ﬁcantly better ﬁt
over Model 3 ( Δχ2=21.56, p=.002). All other models demonstrated
inadequate ﬁt, with signi ﬁcant Bollen –Stine bootstrap χ2values ( p≤
.001), CMIN values of ≥5, and/or CFI of 0.95 or less (see Table 4 ).
The chi-squared difference test indicated that all six multidimensional
models were superior to the unidimensional model.
Regarding factor loadings, for Models 3 and 5, all items loaded
signiﬁcantly onto the respective ﬁrst-order factors, however, in
Model 5, the affective dysregulation factor showed a standardized
indicator loading greater than 1.00. In Model 3, signi ﬁcant corre-
lations were found between all ﬁrst-order factors ranging from r=
0.42 to r=0.90. In Model 5, all ﬁrst-order factors loaded signif-
icantly onto the second-order factors and the correlation between
PTSD and DSO factors was strong ( r=0.99). Factor loadings for
Models 3 and 5 are presented in Table 6 .
Additional cross-validation analyses were conducted by splitting
the data set 70/30 (70% for training and 30% for testing) to assess the
high correlations in Models 3 and 5, identify any overconstrained
parameters, and detect any underlying statistical artifacts. In Model 3,
the correlation between affective dysregulation and disturbances in
relationships in the training set was 0.94 and 0.82 in the test set. This
indicated a consistent performance across training, test, and original
data sets, with all correlations falling below 1.00. In Model 5, thecorrelation between affective dysregulation and DSO in the training
set was 1.06 and 0.94 in the test set. While this indicates improved
correlation in the test set, the training set, and original data set
correlations still indicate multicollinearity or over ﬁtting.
The composite reliability estimates across Models 3 and 5 indi-
cated very similar and acceptable internal reliability for all subscales
apart from the affective dysregulation subscale: reexperiencing =
0.74, avoidance =0.82, sense of threat =0.79 (Model 3) and 0.80
(Model 5), affective dysregulation =0.58, negative self-concept =
0.90, and disturbed relationships =0.83. For Model 5, the PTSD
(0.92) and DSO (0.94) subscales had good internal reliability.
Goodness-of-Fit Analysis for Criterion
A Subset ( n=204)
The results in this subset largely mirrored those obtained in the
full sample, with some differences in goodness-of- ﬁt indices. For the
DSM-5 PTSD models, ﬁt indices were generally better in the subset,
with improved CMIN/ dfand RMSEA values across all models. The
intrusion/distress model demonstrated the best ﬁt within the DSM-5
models in both samples, with CMIN/ df=2.14, RMSEA =.075,
90% CI [.063, .086] for the subset, compared with CMIN/ df=3.39,
RMSEA =.065 in the full sample.
Similarly, the ICD-11 PTSD models showed improved ﬁt in the
subset, particularly for the three-factor model, which continued toTable 5

the six-factor ﬁrst-order model remained the best- ﬁtting model across
both samples, its ﬁt was slightly weaker in the subset (CMIN/ df=
2.00, RMSEA =.07) compared with the full sample (CMIN/ df=
1.47, RMSEA =.03). Conversely, the unidimensional model con-
tinued to show poor ﬁt in both the subset and full sample. These
ﬁndings suggest that model performance is generally consistent
across the full sample and Criterion A subgroup, with slight
improvements in ﬁt indices in the subgroup for simpler models (e.g.,
DSM-5 PTSD and ICD-11 PTSD), but better performance in the full
sample for more complex models (e.g., ICD-11 CPTSD). Further
details on this analysis can be found in Supplemental Table S1 .
This study was the ﬁrst to assess the reliability and ﬁt of several
factorial PTSD and CPTSD models using the PCL-5 and the ITQ in
a sample of parents of autistic children; a population known to
experience higher rates of traumatic parenting experiences and
symptoms than parents of typically developing children ( Stewart et
al., 2020 ). For the PCL-5, two of the ﬁve models adequately ﬁt the
data which was based on: a borderline ﬁt in some of the goodness-
of-ﬁt indicators (e.g., CMIN, CFI, RMSEA), and signi ﬁcant chi-
square values (( Hayduk, 2014a ,2014b ). For the ITQ, the three-
factor PTSD model and both the six-factor ﬁrst-order and two-factorsecond-order CPTSD models based on ICD-11 criteria showed the
bestﬁt to the data. Notably, Model 5 provided a statistically sig-
niﬁcant improvement in ﬁt over Model 3. However, concerns about
overﬁtting and multicollinearity arose in Model 5, with correlations
exceeding 1.00 in the training set and original data set. These
ﬁndings suggest that while Model 5 demonstrates superior statistical
ﬁt, Model 3 offers a more conceptually valid structure, as it avoids
the issues of over ﬁtting. It is important to caution that over ﬁtting in
Model 5 may indicate that the model is too speci ﬁc to this data set
and may not generalize well to other samples. Therefore, further
testing across different data sets is required to ensure the robustness
and applicability of this model. Overall, a good representation of the
latent structure of the ITQ was found in this parent sample, with a
trade-off between statistical ﬁt and conceptual clarity.
This suggests that the ICD-11 models, even the worst among them,
outperform the best DSM-5 models, indicating a superior represen-
tation of PTSD and CPTSD in this parent population. Further support
for this ﬁnding comes from a subset analysis of participants meeting
Criterion A for PTSD ( n=204), which also showed ICD-11 models
demonstrating superior ﬁt compared with DSM-5 models. Both of the
best-ﬁtting ITQ CPTSD models captured the distinction between
PTSD and DSO symptoms inherent to the ICD-11 criteria among
parents of autistic children and as a result were included in the analysis,
despite an item loading of greater than 1 occurring in the affective
dysregulation factor in the two-factor second-order model. As the error
variance in this model was only slightly below zero, this item loading
>1 may have been the result of sampling ﬂuctuations and/or highly
correlated factors, and not necessarily indicative of model mis-
speciﬁcation ( Cooperman & Waller, 2022 ;Kyriazos, 2018 ).Table 6

Further, the ITQ scale scores demonstrated acceptable levels of
internal reliability, with the exception of affective dysregulation.This inadequate reliability was also observed by both Frost et al.
(2022) andSele et al. (2020) , who attributed it to the presence of two
opposing types of emotion regulation coping strategies (i.e., hyper-and hypoaffective states) one of which may be preferred more thanthe other. This theory provides a plausible explanation for the lowerreliability observed in the affective dysregulation subscale in thisstudy and aligns with existing literature on emotional regulationvariability. However, further research is needed to clarify the role ofthese opposing coping strategies and their in ﬂuence on the scale ’s
reliability.
Despite this, all factor loadings for both models were positively
and statistically signi ﬁcant, with mostly moderate to strong load-
ings, indicating that the relationships between the latent factors andthe observed variables were substantial and statistically reliable.
This implies that both models provide a close approximation of the
sample data and represent viable models of the latent PTSD andCPTSD structure of the ITQ. Furthermore, indicating that the ITQmay be an acceptable standardized measurement tool to assessparenting experiences for PTSD and CPTSD speci ﬁcally in parents
of autistic children in both research and clinical contexts. Thisfurther underscores the robustness of the ICD-11 criteria and the
ITQ as superior tools for assessing PTSD and CPTSD in this parentpopulation.
It was interesting to note that only two of the DSM-5 models
provided an adequate ﬁt to the data given the broader symptom
coverage that is inherent in this system ( Hansen et al., 2017 ). The
intrusion/distress model did show a superior ﬁt of the more well-
established DSM-5 PTSD models; however, an overall good ﬁt was
still not demonstrated for this population. The ﬁt of this model may
have been impacted by lower standardized factor loadings occurringfor two scale items (recklessness and trauma-related amnesia) andmay suggest that item modi ﬁcation (e.g., parameter changes, and
item adjustment) may improve the PCL-5 factor structure.
Ourﬁndings clearly indicate that the ICD-11 PTSD criteria, as
assessed by the ITQ, offer a more appropriate and reliable frame-work for trauma assessment and intervention in Australian parentsof autistic children compared with the DSM-5 . While these results
align with the majority of ﬁndings from studies utilizing the
ITQ/ ICD framework, they also highlight unique aspects of trauma
experienced by this speci ﬁc population. The better ﬁto fICD-11
models over DSM-5 models may also be in ﬂuenced by speci ﬁc
sociocultural factors related to our Australian sample. The ongoingcaregiving stressors faced by parents of autistic children, such associal isolation, limited support services, and ﬁnancial strain, might
be particularly pronounced in Australia, potentially shaping howPTSD and CPTSD symptoms cluster in this population. The chronicand cumulative nature of these stressors aligns more closely with
ICD-11 ’s broader framework, which distinguishes between PTSD
and CPTSD. As such, this sample ’s unique experience may con-
tribute to the observed superiority of ICD-11 models, suggesting
that sociocultural context plays an important role in shaping traumaresponses in this parent population.
In particular, our results indicate that the self-report symptoms of
PTSD do not appear to be represented by the traditional four-factormodel (intrusion, avoidance, negative alterations in cognitions andmood, arousal and reactivity) in parents, and instead have a com-plexity that is best understood when looking at PTSD alongsidesymptoms of DSO (e.g., feeling like a failure, worthless, distant or
cutoff, and dif ﬁculties with emotional closeness). Trauma informed
intervention has been reported to appropriately treat symptoms ofDSO (e.g., Purnell et al., 2021 ). This may suggest the need for
clinicians working with some parents of autistic children to utilize
trauma-focused interventions. From a research perspective, futurestudies investigating the ( Norris, 1990 ) mental health of this parent
population may bene ﬁt from considering PTSD and CPTSD con-
ceptualizations in addition to anxiety, depression, and stress.
Limitations and Future Research
A limitation of this study was the fact that only two posttraumatic
stress measures were compared. Other scales (e.g., Traumatic StressSchedule, Norris, 1990 ; and Traumatic Events Questionnaire,
(Hayduk, 2014a ,2014b ;Norris, 1990 ;Vrana & Lauterbach, 1994 )
might also provide adequate conceptualizations of PTSD andCPTSD in this parent population. Additionally, the use of a con-venience sample may have introduced bias, limiting the gener-alizability of the ﬁndings.
The exclusion of the sexual assault item from the LEC-5. Although
sexual assaults are highly correlated with PTSD, this item wasomitted due to the minimal research connecting it to the experience ofparenting trauma in raising autistic children. However, this omissionmay have in ﬂuenced the overall results, as individuals who experi-
enced sexual assault as their traumatic event might not have endorseda Criterion A event.
The potential con ﬂation of trauma, stress, and challenging events in
the full sample was also a consideration. Further analyses isolatingparticipants who met full diagnostic criteria for PTSD or CPTSD,including Criterion A for trauma exposure, demonstrated consistentpsychometric properties within this subgroup. This ﬁnding addresses
concerns regarding potential con ﬂation and reinforces the robustness
of our ﬁndings.
Furthermore, this study relied solely on self-report measures which
can lead to discrepancies between reported symptoms those identi ﬁed
in clinical interviews. This reliance on self-report may have affectedthe reported prevalence and presentation of PTSD. Future researchshould consider complementing self-report measures with structuredclinical interviews to provide a more comprehensive understanding ofPTSD symptoms in this population.
Finally, it is important to highlight that this study did not assess
the diagnostic accuracy of the PTSD measures used in this speci ﬁc
population. While tools provided valuable insights into PTSD andCPTSD prevalence, their accuracy in diagnosing these conditionsamong parents of autistic children remains untested.
Future studies should explore a broader range of trauma measures
beyond the PCL-5 and ITQ to capture a more comprehensiveunderstanding of PTSD and CPTSD in parents of autistic children.This could include scales such as the Traumatic Stress Schedule andthe Traumatic Events Questionnaire. Additionally, to enhance thegeneralizability of ﬁndings, future research should employ random
sampling methods rather than convenience sampling, ensuring amore representative sample of the parent population. Furthermore, itis crucial to further investigate the role of DSO symptoms in themanifestation of PTSD and CPTSD among parents of autisticchildren. The strong representation of DSO in the ICD-11 models
and the inadequacy of traditional PTSD models in capturing thecomplexity of symptoms in this population suggest that a deeper1032 HINDE ET AL.
understanding of DSO could inform the development of more
targeted and effective therapeutic strategies.
The present study is the ﬁrst to our knowledge to examine the ﬁt
of data from a population of Australian parents of autistic children tovarious DSM-5 and ICD-11 models of PTSD and CPTSD,
respectively, as measured by the ITQ and the PCL-5. Empiricalsupport was found for the three-factor ICD-11 PTSD model, as well
as the six-factor ﬁrst-order and the two-factor second-order ICD-11
CPTSD models when using the ITQ, suggesting that the factorstructure of the ITQ may be a valid screening assessment for PTSDand CPTSD in this parent population. Additional analysis of Models3 and 5 revealed that Model 5 provided a signi ﬁcantly better sta-
tistical ﬁt; however, concerns over multicollinearity and over ﬁtting,
indicated by correlations exceeding 1.00 in the training set andoriginal data set, suggesting that Model 3 may offer a more con-ceptually valid structure. While Model 3 captures a better statisticalﬁt, the trade-off with Model 3 ’s conceptual clarity should be
considered. Two of the DSM-5 models tested using the PCL-5
showed an adequate ﬁt to the data with the remaining three models
providing a poor ﬁt. The novel intrusion/distress model showed a
superior ﬁt compared with the other four DSM-5 models tested.
While the results support the factorial validity and internal reliabilityof both the PCL-5 and the ITQ, the ITQ may provide a more validPTSD and CPTSD measure for this parent population.

Accepted April 17, 2025 ▪POSTTRAUMATIC STRESS SYMPTOMS 1035

ICD-11 PTSD, Complex PTSD, and Substance Use Disorder:
Associations and Clinical Correlates
Logan R. Harvey1, 2, Tim Slade1, Christina Marel1, and Katherine L. Mills1
1The Matilda Centre, The University of Sydney
2Drug Health, Western Sydney Local Health District, Sydney, Australia
Objective: Trauma- and substance-related disorders are highly comorbid. The co-occurrence of these
conditions is associated with a more severe clinical pro ﬁle in patients, and this remains a signi ﬁcant
challenge for treatment providers. Previous research examining this comorbidity has focused on
Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition, posttraumatic stress disorder
(PTSD), resulting in a signi ﬁcant lack of research examining other formulations of trauma-related
disorders, in particular complex posttraumatic stress disorder (CPTSD). It remains unclear whether
CPTSD requires additional treatment approaches in substance use treatment settings. The present study
sought to expand previous research by examining the International Classi ﬁcation of Diseases
11th revision PTSD and Complex PTSD (CPTSD) diagnoses in a substance use treatment setting, and to
explore the associations between symptoms of PTSD/CPTSD and characteristics of substance use
disorder. Method: The study utilized data collected from individuals commencing substance use
treatment ( n=72). Assessments included trauma exposure, trauma-related symptoms, primary substance
of concern, psychological dependence, and recent substance use. Results: Forty percent of the sample
screened positive for a trauma-related disorder, with CPTSD more prevalent (34.8%) than PTSD (5.8%).
There was limited evidence of associations between trauma-related symptoms and substance use
characteristics. Severity of each CPTSD symptom cluster (affect dysregulation, negative self-concept,
disturbances in relationships) was signi ﬁcantly, positively associated with psychological dependence, a
pattern not found for PTSD symptoms. Conclusions: Given that CPTSD is more prevalent and
demonstrates differential relationships to substance use characteristics, future research should examine
whether individuals with comorbid CPTSD would bene ﬁt from adjusted treatment approaches.
Clinical Impact Statement
Individuals presenting for substance use treatment demonstrate high levels of exposure to traumatic
events, and an increased prevalence of trauma-related disorders. Complex posttraumatic stress disorder
(CPTSD) is more prevalent than PTSD in this population. There appear to be differential associations
between symptoms of PTSD versus CPTSD and psychological dependence to a substance. The
additional symptoms associated with CPTSD should be considered in treatment —including via direct
screening at assessment. Existing, evidence-based treatments should be adapted to address the additional
CPTSD-related symptoms within substance use treatment.
Keywords: posttraumatic stress disorder, complex posttraumatic stress disorder, trauma, substance-related
disorders, addiction

Psychological Trauma:
Theory, Research, Practice, and Policy
2025 The Author(s) 2026, Vol. 18, No. 5, 1036 –1044
ISSN: 1942-9681 https://doi.org/10.1037/tra0001957
For individuals presenting for treatment of substance use disorder
(SUD), trauma-related disorders are a prevalent comorbidity. InAustralia, it is estimated that between 5 and 66% of those presentingfor substance use treatment will meet criteria for current posttrau-matic stress disorder ( Kingston et al., 2017 ) with the predominance
of studies deriving estimates that are considerably higher than thoseobserved in the general population (4.4%; McEvoy et al., 2011 ).
Individuals presenting with this comorbidity are more likely toexperience additional comorbid mental and physical health concerns(Bowe & Rosenheck, 2015 ;Prior et al., 2017 ), greater severity of
SUD ( Patel et al., 2021 ), and more signi ﬁcant functional and
psychosocial impairment ( L. R. Cohen & Hien, 2006 ;Drapkin et al.,
2011). Consistent with this, PTSD has been identi ﬁed as a key
treatment challenge for substance use treatment providers ( Gielen
et al., 2014 ;Killeen et al., 2015 ;Mefodeva et al., 2023 ;Roberts
et al., 2023 ).
The co-occurrence of SUD and PTSD has most commonly been
understood in terms of the self-medication hypothesis —the theory
that substance use functions to relieve symptoms of PTSD ( Khantzian,
1997). In line with this, symptoms of PTSD and SUD are mutually
reinforcing ( Tripp et al., 2020 ), and individuals with this comorbidity
experience cravings in response to both trauma- and substance-relatedcues ( Coffey et al., 2002 ). Individuals with co-occurring PTSD and
SUD experience worse clinical outcomes compared with those withsingle diagnoses, particularly in relation to functional and psychosocialimpairments ( L. R. Cohen & Hien, 2006 ;Najt et al., 2011 ). Effective
treatments do exist; however, these demonstrate high levels of attrition(Hien et al., 2023 ;Roberts et al., 2022 ). These treatments typically
involve the parallel or integrated targeting of both PTSD- and SUD-related symptoms. In general, trauma-focused interventions areeffective in the presence of co-occurring substance use and there isevidence that reductions in trauma-related symptoms correspondto reductions in SUD-related symptoms —highlighting the sig-
niﬁcance of targeting this comorbidity within SUD treatment
(Back et al., 2014 ;Hien et al., 2010 ,2018 ;Kaczkurkin et al., 2016 ;
Roberts et al., 2023 ). The available literature indicates that pathways
to recovery from substance use are often nonlinear, and there havebeen limited ﬁndings to date which help identify individuals who
might be treatment responders versus those who do not ( Lopez-Castro
et al., 2015 ). Given the increased symptom severity and high rates
of treatment attrition in this presentation, it remains important tofurther understand the relationship between PTSD and SUD to informreﬁnements to treatment options.
The majority of studies that have examined this relationship have
focused on hyperarousal symptoms. These studies have found pos-itive associations between arousal/hyperarousal symptoms andmeasures of substance use including age of onset, duration of SUD,number of substances used, patterns of recent substance use, andpharmacotherapy dosage ( Dell’Osso et al., 2014 ;Love & Torgerson,
2019;Shipherd et al., 2005 ;Stewart et al., 1999
;Tull et al., 2010 ). In a
systematic review of the comorbidity between PTSD and alcoholmisuse, Debell et al. (2014) reported a consistent positive association
between arousal/hyperarousal and alcohol misuse, and other studieshave suggested a similar relationship for anxiolytics, analgesics(Stewart et al., 1999 ), cocaine, and cannabis ( Khoury et al., 2010 ), but
not for crack/cocaine ( Tull et al., 2010 )o rh e r o i n( Khoury et al., 2010 ;
Tull et al., 2010 ). Symptoms of avoidance and numbing have been
associated with broad categories of alcohol and other drug use, albeitwith even less consistency in results ( Boland et al., 2018 ;Love &Torgerson, 2019 ;McFall et al., 1992 ). Regarding speci ﬁcs u b s t a n c e s ,
symptoms of avoidance/numbing were identi ﬁed in the Debell et al. ’s
(2014) review as consistently associated with alcohol misuse, while
positive associations have also been identi ﬁed with anxiolytic use
(Stewart et al., 1999 ), cocaine use, and cannabis use ( Khoury et al.,
2010). In contrast, Tull et al. (2010) did not identify a relationship
between crack/cocaine use and avoidance/numbing symptoms but dididentify a negative association between avoidance/numbing andheroin use. Negative alterations in cognitions and mood were found tobe associated with alcohol use in one study of U.S. veterans ( Boland
et al., 2018 ), but this result was not replicated in a nonclinical sample
which similarly did not identify a relationship between a broad “other
substances ”(i.e., nonalcohol) category and negative alterations in
mood and thinking ( Love & Torgerson, 2019 ). Finally, reexperien-
cing symptoms have been found to be associated with alcohol ( Debell
et al., 2014 ;Love & Torgerson, 2019 ) and other drug use ( McFall
et al., 1992 ) including cocaine and cannabis ( Khoury et al., 2010 ), and
analgesics ( Stewart et al., 1999 ).
Importantly, the relationships identi ﬁed in these studies have not
been consistently replicated across studies. This may be due in partto differential endorsement of individual symptoms across differentsamples but also to the inclusion of different measures of differentiterations of the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental
Disorders (DSM ) PTSD symptom formulations across time (e.g.,
Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Third
Edition, to Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders,
Fifth Edition, Text Revision). As diagnostic systems have devel-oped, further research is required to expand current knowledge. Inaddition to PTSD, the International Classi ﬁcation of Diseases
11th revision (ICD-11 ;World Health Organization, 2019 ) has
included a new diagnosis of Complex PTSD (CPTSD), whichprovides an opportunity to reexamine the above relationships butalso expand these to the new CPTSD diagnosis. This is importantalso as there is a signi ﬁcant lack of research examining the rela-
tionship between
ICD trauma-related disorders and SUDs in gen-
eral. Complex PTSD in this formulation requires an individual toﬁrst meet the criteria for PTSD (reexperiencing symptoms, avoidance
symptoms, and persistent sense of threat) but also to demonstratethe experience of three other symptom clusters re ﬂecting disturbances
in self-organization (affect dysregulation, negative self-concept, and
difﬁculties in relationships). Given this, it is possible that the
additional symptom clusters may help further explain the rela-tionship between trauma-related pathology and SUD, including intreatment. For example, dif ﬁculties managing and regulating emo-
tions (as per the CPTSD symptom cluster of emotion regulationdifﬁculties) have been found to be associated with less substance use
treatment completed and to also be a maintaining factor in substanceuse disorder ( Cheetham et al., 2010 ;Tull et al., 2013 ).
TheICD-11 CPTSD diagnosis has been found to most commonly
proceed the experience of cumulative, childhood, or interpersonal
trauma ( Frost et al., 2019 ;Maercker et al., 2022 ;Palic et al., 2016 ;
Powers et al., 2017 ) and is associated with higher levels of symptom
severity, functional impairment, lower levels of psychological well-being, and greater levels of co-occurring mental health problems thanthe PTSD diagnosis ( Bressington et al., 2024 ;Cloitre et al., 2019 ;
Howard et al., 2021 ;Murphy et al., 2021 ). This includes higher levels
of loneliness, sleep problems, use of psychotropic medications, andsuicidality ( Folke et al., 2023 ;McGinty et al., 2024 ). Importantly,
these patterns of trauma exposure and comorbidity are commonlyICD-11 PTSD, CPTSD, AND SUBSTANCE USE 1037
seen among people with SUD ( D i e h le ta l . ,2 0 1 9 ;Ruglass et al., 2017 ;
Santo et al., 2021 ). Within psychiatric clinical populations (compared
with community samples), there is a higher prevalence of CPTSDthan PTSD ( Maercker et al., 2022 ). For example, in an Australian
sample of treatment seeking veterans, Bressington et al. (2024)
identi ﬁed signi ﬁcantly higher rates of CPTSD versus PTSD (51.4%
vs. 9.1%). However, the prevalence and clinical correlates of CPTSDamong people in substance use treatment services have yet to beexamined. Early indicators suggest that there is a strong comorbiditybetween CPTSD and SUD. Powers et al. (2017) found that
Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fourth
Edition, Text Revision, alcohol abuse and dependence were moreprevalent in an ICD-11 CPTSD group than a DSM-5 PTSD group,
although Karatzias, Hyland, et al. (2019) found similarly high rates of
AUD in individuals with ICD-11 PTSD and CPTSD (60.0% vs.
58.8%). Finally, Facer-Irwin et al. (2022) found that those with ICD-
11PTSD were more likely to screen positive for alcohol dependence
(OR=3.2), while those with ICD-11 CPTSD were more likely to
screen positive for other substance use ( OR=3 . 4 ) .I ti sd i f ﬁcult to
draw conclusions from these results, however, as only a small numberof studies exist and they compare different diagnostic systems. Thereis emerging evidence that existing treatments for PTSD may have aneffect on symptoms of CPTSD ( Karatzias, Murphy, et al., 2019 ),
however, no studies to date have evaluated the treatment of comorbidCPTSD –SUD, and it is unclear whether the existing literature related
to PTSD can be generalized to this group. Importantly also, forexisting PTSD –SUD treatments, clinically meaningful improvements
are only demonstrated for approximately half of those who undertakethem ( Mills et al., 2016 ). Given this and the fact that the majority of
research to date has focused on the core symptoms of PTSD, it remainsunclear whether the additional symptom clusters associated withCPTSD will further understandings of treatment options. Based onexisting research in other clinical populations, it is likely that CPTSDwill be prevalent in this population, and it is therefore crucial toevaluate the relationship between CPTSD and SUD.
The Present Study
The aim of the present study then, was to expand previous
research by ﬁrst examining the prevalence of ICD-11 PTSD and
CPTSD in a substance use treatment setting, and second to explorethe associations between ICD-11 trauma-related symptom clusters
and characteristics of SUD (primary substance of concern, recentsubstance use, and psychological dependence). As most previousresearch has focused on substance use behavior or on speci ﬁc
substances, the present study will additionally examine psycho-logical dependence to a substance. Psychological dependence is animportant characteristic of substance use disorders and re ﬂects the
nonphysiological components of dependence to a psychoactivesubstance ( Raistrick et al., 1994 ). This component of dependence
is reﬂected in diagnostic formulations for substance-related con-
ditions across both DSM andICD systems.
Materials and Method
The present study analyzed data from the Trauma and Substance
Use in Treatment study, a prospective cohort study of 72 peoplerecruited from a large, public metropolitan substance use treatment
service based in greater Sydney, Australia. Ethics approval wasobtained from the Western Sydney Local Health District HumanResearch Ethics Committee (HREC/18/WMEAD/441).
Participants were recruited as they entered substance use treat-
ment. All eligible patients entering treatment with a counselor orpsychologist for substance use issues were invited to participate.
Eligibility criteria were as follows:(a) aged 18 years or over; (b) Australian citizen or permanent
resident; (c) ﬂuent in English; and (d) agreed to provide contact
details for follow-up.
Participants were administered a series of measures by their
treating clinician in their initial clinical assessment sessions. Thesemeasures assessed demographics, substance use (past month), traumaexposure (lifetime), and trauma symptoms (past month).
Demographics
Demographics included participant age (years) and gender
(“male, ”“female, ”“transgender, ”“do not identify as female, male
or transgender, ”or“prefer not to say ”).
Substance Use
Substance use characteristics were assessed as part of routine
data collection upon treatment entry using the NSW Health Drugand Alcohol Clinical Assessment and the Australian TreatmentOutcomes Pro ﬁle (Ryan et al., 2014 ). This included self-identi ﬁed
primary substance of concern and secondary substance of concern(categorized as “alcohol, ”“cannabis, ”or“other illicit substance, ”
the latter group consisting of cocaine, heroin, and methamphet-amine); and the number of days in the past month they had used theirprimary substance (maximum of 28 days). The Leeds DependenceQuestionnaire (LDQ; Raistrick et al., 1994 ) was used to assess
psychological dependence in relation to primary substance of concern.The LDQ is a 10-item self-report measure of psychological depen-dence to psychoactive substances. Scores range from 0 to 30, withhigher scores indicating a greater level of psychological dependence.The LDQ has been found to have sound psychometric properties,including reliability, validity, and sensitivity to change ( Heather et al.,
2001;Raistrick et al., 2014 ). In the present study, the LDQ demon-
strated sound reliability ( α=.93).
Trauma, PTSD, and CPTSD
The Childhood Trauma Questionnaire (CTQ) was used to assess
trauma exposure speci ﬁcally in childhood ( Bernstein & Fink,
1998 ). The CTQ is a 28-item measure that assesses exposure to ﬁve
domains of childhood maltreatment: physical abuse, sexual abuse,emotional abuse, physical neglect, and emotional neglect. Scoresare summed for each domain, with higher scores re ﬂecting more
severe levels of maltreatment. Endorsement of each domain ofchildhood maltreatment was calculated based on the suggested cutscores for the measure ( Bernstein & Fink, 1998 ). The Life Events
Checklist for DSM-5 (LEC5; Weathers et al., 2013 ) was admin-
istered to assess trauma exposure more broadly. In considering1038 HARVEY, SLADE, MAREL, AND MILLS
exposure to LEC-5 —only those directly experienced were considered
for this particular study. The International Trauma Questionnaire
(ITQ; Cloitre et al., 2018 ) was used to assess symptoms of ICD-11
PTSD and CPTSD. Participant responses to the ITQ were scored as
per the recommended diagnostic algorithm ( Cloitre et al., 2018 ).
Speci ﬁcally, participants who endorsed an individual symptom at
“moderately ”or higher on at least one item assessing the symptom
were considered to endorse the symptom. For participants to screen
positive for ICD-11 PTSD, they were required to endorse one or more
symptoms from each of the three PTSD core symptom clusters (re-
experiencing, avoidance, and persistent sense of threat) and functional
impairment related to those symptoms. For ICD-11 CPTSD, parti-
cipants were required to meet criteria for the PTSD diagnosis and
additionally endorse one or more symptoms from each of the three
CPTSD symptom clusters (affect regulation issues, negative self-
concept, and interpersonal issues) and functional impairment related
to those symptoms. A summed score for each symptom cluster was
calculated to act as a measure of symptom severity. The ITQ has been
validated in a range of populations and settings and demonstrates
sound psychometric properties ( Redican et al., 2021 ). In the present
study, the ITQ demonstrated sound reliability for the full scale ( α=
.94) PTSD scale ( α=.82) and CPTSD subscale ( α=.88).
Data Analysis
There was a small amount ( <1%) of missing data across the survey
questions. Numbers are reported alongside proportions for categorical
variables broken down by group for demographic, substance use, and
trauma variables. The distributions of all continuous variables to be
examined inferentially were found to deviate from normality, with
signiﬁcant issues with skewness evident as assessed with the Shapiro –
Wilk and Kolmogorov –Smirnov tests and by visual inspection of
Q–Q plots. Medians are reported with interquartile ranges (IQR) for
continuous variables. Inferential analyses were carried out using
median scores primarily, however, means and standard deviations are
additionally reported for key study variables to allow comparison to
the broader literature.
To evaluate the associations between primary substance groups
and the trauma-related symptom clusters, Kruskal –Wallis and
Mann–Whitney tests were utilized. For the Kruskal –Wallis test,
effect sizes were calculated using η-squared calculated for the H
statistic ( η2;B. H. Cohen, 2008 ;Tomczak & Tomczak, 2014 ). This
effect size re ﬂects the variance in mean rank accounted for by the
group variable, analogous to the eta-squared used within a simple
analysis of variance ( B. H. Cohen, 2008 ). These effect sizes were
interpreted with the available convention for the standard η2based
on the fstatistics, where small =.01, medium =.06, and large =.14
(J. Cohen, 1988 ). Pairwise comparisons were then conducted with
Mann–Whitney Utests utilizing a Bonferroni correction for
multiple comparisons, with Glass ’s rank-biserial correlation ( rG)
used to estimate effect sizes ( Glass, 1966 ;Tomczak & Tomczak,
2014 ). This effect size provides a measure of the difference in
average rank between two groups and is similar to the Pearson r
statistic ( B. H. Cohen, 2008 ). These effect sizes were interpreted
based on the available conventions, with small =.1, medium =.3,
and large =.5 (J. Cohen, 1988 ). To assess associations between
symptom clusters and the other substance use characteristics (recent
substance use, psychological dependence), correlation coef ﬁcients
were calculated using Spearman ’s rank-order correlation ( rs).Results
Sample Characteristics
Sixty-one percent of the sample identi ﬁed as male ( n=44,
61.1%) with the remainder identifying as female ( n=28, 38.9%).
The mean age was 35.78 ( SD=10.09). Half the sample had received
prior substance use treatment ( n=40, 55.6%).
Alcohol was the most common primary drug of concern ( n=29,
40.3%); cannabis was endorsed as the primary drug of concern for
20.8% ( n=15). Thirty-nine percent ( n=28) endorsed other illicit
drugs as their primary drug of concern, predominantly metham-
phetamine (n =26, 36.1%). Other drugs included in this category were
cocaine ( n=1; 1.4%) and heroin ( n=1, 1.4%). Just over one third of
the sample also identi ﬁed a secondary substance of concern ( n=25,
34.7%). Most participants reported using their primary substance in
the 28 days preceding the assessment (70.3%), with the median
number of days of recent substance use being 7.5 (IQR =0.00, 22.50).
Trauma Exposure
One hundred percent of the sample had experienced at least one
domain of childhood maltreatment (median of 3.5 different types
IQR=2.00, 4.00) and 94.6% had been exposed to a traumatic event
as measured by the LEC5. The most prevalent domain of childhood
maltreatment was physical neglect ( n=70, 98.6%), followed by
emotional abuse ( n=50, 70.4%), emotional neglect ( n=47,
67.1%), physical abuse ( n=41, 57.7%), and sexual abuse ( n=24,
33.8%). The median number of LEC5 events experienced was 4.00
(IQR =2.00, 5.75). Rates of exposure to events on the LEC5 are
presented in Table 1 .

Associations Between Trauma-Related Symptoms and
Substance Use Characteristics
Tables 3 and4presents the median ITQ symptom cluster scores,
across primary substance groups. The results of group comparisons
for primary substance are presented from the Kruskal –Wallis test,
along with pairwise comparisons utilizing the Mann –Whitney U
No statistically signi ﬁcant group differences were identi ﬁed
regarding the ITQ symptom clusters, and all effect sizes were
small. Exploratory pairwise comparisons were similarly nonsig-
niﬁcant, although medium sized effects were found when com-
paring the alcohol and cannabis groups in regard to each functional
impairment symptom cluster and the disturbance in relationships
symptom cluster.
Table 5 presents the correlation matrix for the remaining sub-
stance use variables (psychological dependence and recent sub-
stance use) and ITQ symptom cluster scores. There was no evidence
of association between any of the ITQ symptom cluster scores and
recent substance use. There were relationships identi ﬁed between
ITQ CPTSD symptom clusters and psychological dependence, withsigniﬁcant, medium sized associations identi ﬁed between psycho-
logical dependence and affect dysregulation, rs(68)=.35,p=.003,
negative self-concept, rs(68) =.38,p=.002, disturbances in re-
lationships, rs(66) =.43,p<.001, and CPTSD-related functional
impairment, rs(68) =.43,p≤.001.
This study was one of the ﬁrst to examine the prevalence and
clinical correlates of ICD-11 PTSD and CPTSD in a population
seeking substance use treatment. As expected, the sample dem-
onstrated high rates of exposure to traumatic experiences across
the lifespan. Unsurprisingly, there were correspondingly high rates
of trauma-related symptoms identi ﬁed. Rates of endorsement for
each of the ICD-11 PTSD and CPTSD symptoms were above 50%,
and over 40% of the sample screened positive for either trauma-
related disorder. Of speci ﬁc importance, rates of CPTSD were
found to be considerably higher than PTSD. There are no previ-
ously published rates of ICD-11 PTSD or CPTSD in individuals
presenting for substance use treatment by which the current results
could be better understood. Broadly, the overall prevalence of
40.6% of the sample screening positive for either disorder is in line
with earlier prevalence rates of PTSD in comparable samples
(Kingston et al., 2017 ). The ﬁnding that the majority of these
individuals (85.7%) re ﬂected a CPTSD rather than a PTSD pro ﬁle
is signi ﬁcant and suggests that CPTSD is a particularly salient
clinical issue in substance use treatment. This ﬁnding is consistent
with other studies conducted among clinical samples. For example,
previous research in individuals seeking trauma-related treatment
at a public psychiatric service in Scotland found that 75.6% of
individuals re ﬂected a CPTSD pro ﬁle, and only 24.4% endorsed
aP T S Dp r o ﬁle (Karatzias et al., 2017 ). The rates of CPTSD
symptoms present in the current sample provide an important
indicator for clinicians that as sessment of posttraumatic psycho-
pathology needs to be expanded beyond the well-established
traditional symptoms of standard PTSD.
Although con ﬁrming the expectedly high rates of CPTSD in this
sample, this study found limited evidence of differences between
users of particular substances in regard to symptoms of PTSD and
CPTSD. There was indication of a trend toward higher levels of
PTSD and CPTSD-related functional impairment and problems in
relationships in those who nominated alcohol rather than cannabis as
their main drug of concern. This could suggest that there may beTable 2

variability in the symptom pro ﬁles and type of treatment required by
different substance groups. Further research is required to explore
this trend. Previous research has primarily found evidence of an
association between substance use and hyperarousal symptoms,
although there is considerable uncertainty across results and these
associations have not been examined in the context of the ICD-11 .
This study did not identify any signi ﬁcant relationships between
recent substance use and symptoms of PTSD or CPTSD. This may
be an artifact of the current sample characteristics, or low power,
however, previous studies have found inconsistent results in this
area—indicating that the association between current trauma-related
symptoms and recent substance use is not clear. For example,
Garrison-Desany et al. (2024) identi ﬁed associations between
recent nicotine and alcohol use and PTSD, but did not identify this
association for cannabis use. Results such as these highlight the
complexity of the relationship between PTSD and SUD.
Importantly, all of the CPTSD symptom clusters were found to
have moderate relationships to psychological dependence, a rela-
tionship not found for any of the PTSD symptom clusters. This study
was the ﬁrst to examine the relationships between speci ﬁc PTSD
and CPTSD symptom clusters and psychological dependence, but
previous research has in general indicated greater levels of comorbidsymptoms in individuals with CPTSD compared with PTSD
(Maercker et al., 2022 ). This ﬁnding also highlights the bene ﬁts of
looking at SUD beyond substance use behavior and considering in
more detail the associations between trauma- and substance-related
symptoms. Future research should expand on this and include
measures of dependence constructs or varied measures of substance
use symptoms to further elucidate associations.
Theﬁndings of this study support the notion that unique or
additional treatment approaches may be needed to target-speci ﬁc
CPTSD symptoms ( Karatzias & Cloitre, 2019 ;Karatzias, Murphy,
et al., 2019 ). To date, no studies have speci ﬁcally examined the
treatment of comorbid SUD and CPTSD. The existing literature
provides strong evidence for concurrent, trauma-focused treatments
for comorbid PTSD and SUD ( Roberts et al., 2022 ,2023 ). This
includes the use of integrated CBT programs utilizing Prolonged
Exposure such as Concurrent Treatment of Substance Use Disorders
Using Prolonged Exposure (Back et al., 2015 ). Given that programs
such as this have demonstrated good effects on symptoms of PTSD
and SUD in general, clinicians should consider implementing these
interventions but also consider personalizing this to address potential
additional treatment needs for those experiencing CPTSD symptoms.

*p<.05. **p<.01.ICD-11 PTSD, CPTSD, AND SUBSTANCE USE 1041
effect on CPTSD symptoms ( Karatzias, Murphy, et al., 2019 )a n d
there are existing examples of using modular treatment approaches toaddress symptoms of CPTSD alongside those of PTSD, such as theEnhanced STAIR program ( Karatzias et al., 2024 ). In this way,
clinicians should consider building on or adapting existing treatmentsin a deliberate way to speci ﬁcally address symptoms of CPTSD.
Limitations
There are several limitations that should be considered when
reviewing the current results. Primarily, the emergence of a globalpandemic and the redirection of health service resources, necessi-tated the premature cessation of recruitment for the study. Thesubsequent sample size will have in ﬂuenced statistical power and
the generalizability of results. In particular, the small sample andunequal group sizes precluded group comparisons for the PTSDand CPTSD groups. The present study is also only able to providescreened estimates of prevalence rates for PTSD and CPTSD, as nodiagnostic con ﬁrmation was obtained via clinical interview. There
are, however, strong suggestions that diagnostic con ﬁrmation via
clinical interview should not always be considered superior in thisendeavor ( Hyland & Shevlin, 2024 ). There remains a strong need for
further research in this area. Given the complexities of collectingdata in under resourced, frontline services, it is important that futureresearch employ mechanisms to maximize the utility of data col-lected. The examination of a broader range of substance use char-acteristics, including primary substances of concern, will allowmore detailed analysis of the relationships between trauma- andsubstance-related disorders.
In conclusion, the present study con ﬁrms the high prevalence
of trauma exposure in individuals seeking treatment for substanceuse problems but also expands the literature by providing the ﬁrst
estimates of the rates of ICD-11 PTSD and CPTSD in this
population. These results suggest that CPTSD is a potentiallyimportant comorbid diagnosis in SUD treatment requiring furtherexamination.

International meta-analysis of PTSD genome-wide
association studies identi ﬁes sex- and ancestry-
speci ﬁc genetic risk loci
Caroline M. Nievergelt et al.#
The risk of posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) following trauma is heritable, but robust
common variants have yet to be identi ﬁed. In a multi-ethnic cohort including over 30,000
PTSD cases and 170,000 controls we conduct a genome-wide association study of PTSD. Wedemonstrate SNP-based heritability estimates of 5 –20%, varying by sex. Three genome-wide
signiﬁcant loci are identi ﬁed, 2 in European and 1 in African-ancestry analyses. Analyses
strati ﬁed by sex implicate 3 additional loci in men. Along with other novel genes and
non-coding RNAs, a Parkinson ’s disease gene involved in dopamine regulation, PARK2 ,i s
associated with PTSD. Finally, we demonstrate that polygenic risk for PTSD is signi ﬁcantly
predictive of re-experiencing symptoms in the Million Veteran Program dataset, althoughspeci ﬁc loci did not replicate. These results demonstrate the role of genetic variation in the
biology of risk for PTSD and highlight the necessity of conducting sex-strati ﬁed analyses and
expanding GWAS beyond European ancestry populations.https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-019-12576-w OPEN
#A full list of authors and their af ﬁliations appears at the end of the paper.
NATURE COMMUNICATIONS | (2019) 10:4558 | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-019-12576-w | www.nature.com/naturecommunications 11234567890():,;
Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) is a commonly
occurring mental health consequence of exposure to
extreme, life threatening stress, and/or serious injury/harm.
PTSD is frequently associated with the occurrence of comorbidmental disorders such as major depression
1and other adverse
health sequelae including type 2 diabetes and cardiovascular
disease2,3. Given this high prevalence and impact, PTSD is a
serious public health problem. An understanding of the biological
mechanisms of risk for PTSD is therefore an important goal of
research ultimately aimed at its prevention and mitigation4,5.
Exposure to traumatic stress is, by de ﬁnition, requisite for the
development of PTSD, but individual susceptibility to PTSD
(conditioned on trauma exposure) varies widely. Twin studies
over the past two decades provide persuasive evidence for at least
some genetic in ﬂuence on PTSD risk6,7, and the last decade has
witnessed the beginnings of a concerted effort to detect speci ﬁc
genetic susceptibility variants for PTSD8,9.
The Psychiatric Genomics Consortium —PTSD Group (PGC-
PTSD) published results from a large GWAS on PTSD, involving
a trans-ethnic sample of over 20,000 individuals, approximately
5000 (25%) of whom were cases10. With this limited sample size,
no individual variants exceeded genome-wide signi ﬁcance; how-
ever, signi ﬁcant estimates of SNP heritability and genetic corre-
lations between PTSD and other mental disorders such as
schizophrenia were demonstrated for the ﬁrst time.
It is apparent from previous PGC work on other mental dis-
orders that sample size is paramount for GWAS to discern
common genome-wide signi ﬁcant variants of small effect that are
replicable11. Subsequent to the publication of data from the ﬁrst
freeze10, the PGC-PTSD has continued to acquire additional
PTSD cases and controls through partnerships with an expanding
network of investigators, such that we now have accrued a sample
size that has enabled us to turn the corner on genome-wide risk
discovery. Presented here are the results of our latest GWA stu-
dies that include over 23,000 European and over 4000 African
ancestry PTSD cases, now involving a total trans-ethnic sample of
over 200,000 individuals. In achieving this sample size, weidentify sex- and ancestry-speci ﬁcﬁndings. GWAS and gene-
based analyses across our cohorts indicate genome-wide sig-
niﬁcant associations, involving genes related to dopamine and
immune pathways. We show high genetic correlations between
PTSD and related psychiatric disorders such as major depressive
disorder, but present evidence that some of the identi ﬁed loci arelikely speci ﬁc to PTSD. In addition, we construct a highly sig-
niﬁcant polygenic risk score for PTSD, which is predictive of re-
experiencing symptoms (REX), a core feature of PTSD, in the
independent Million Veteran Program cohort
Meta-analysis strategy across ancestries and sex . We report
meta-analyses of GWAS from the PGC-PTSD Freeze 2 (PGC2),
comprised of an ancestrally diverse group of 206,655 participants
(including 32,428 cases) from 60 different PTSD studies, ranging
from clinically deeply characterized, small patient groups to largecohorts with self-reported PTSD symptoms (Supplementary
Data 1). Trauma exposure included both civilian and/or military
events, often with pre-existing exposure to childhood trauma, and
the majority of controls were trauma-exposed. First ancestry
groups were consistently de ﬁned across studies (Supplementary
Fig. 1). Primary GWAS were then performed separately in the
three largest ancestry groups (European (EUA), African (AFA),
and Native American Ancestry (AMA)), then meta-analyzed
across studies and ancestry groups. Given the previously observed
differences between male and female heritability estimates in
PGC-PTSD Freeze 1
10, we also performed sex-strati ﬁed analyses.
Quantile-quantile plots showed low in ﬂation across analyses
(Supplementary Fig. 2), which was mostly accounted for by
polygenic SNP effects with little indication of residual population
strati ﬁcation (see Supplementary Note 1 for additional
information).
Heritability of PTSD . We estimated heritability of PTSD in the
EUA studies (Table 1) based on information captured by geno-
typing arrays ( h2SNP) from summary statistics using LDSC12.
Assuming a population prevalence of 30% after trauma exposure,
overall h2SNPin PGC2 was 0.05 on the liability scale ( P=3.18
10−8). However, female heritability was highly signi ﬁcant
(h2SNP=0.10, P=8.03  10−11), while male heritability was not
signiﬁcantly different from zero ( h2SNP=0.01, P=0.63).
We further examined sex differences in heritability in different
subsets of the data: the PGC2 data without the UK Biobank
(referred to as PGC1.5) and the UK Biobank (UKB) by itself,
which comprises a large proportion of PGC2. Similar to the
overall PGC2, heritability in PGC1.5 was high in women and
not signi ﬁcant in men. In contrast, in the UKB, male heritability

We also compared heritability across ancestries using the
GCTA GREML method, which allows analysis of admixed
populations when individual genotypes are available. GCTA
estimates in the smaller EUA data remained similar to LDSC onthe full data (Table 2). Heritability in AFA was comparable to
estimates for EUA in the overall sample and when strati ﬁed by
Comparability of PGC2 studies and sex-speci ﬁc analyses .A
comparison of heritability estimates in subsets of PGC2 studies
strati ﬁed by sex, ancestry, and characteristics of study (i.e.
PGC1.5 vs. the large UKB cohort) show signi ﬁcant estimates for
PTSD in the range of 10 –20% (Tables 1–2). This is with the
notable exception of PGC1.5 males (in EUA and AFA analyses),
which fail to show signi ﬁcant h
2SNP, despite similar numbers of
PTSD cases compared to PGC1.5 women. To further evaluate the
comparability of PGC2 studies we estimated genetic correlations
(rg) between subsets with different characteristics (Supplementary
As numerous small studies contribute to PGC1.5 (24 EUA
studies with N< 200 cases, Supplementary Table 3), we ﬁrst
investigated the contribution of small studies to the overall
genetic signal and genetic similarity to the larger PGC1.5 cohorts.
The combined subset of small studies showed signi ﬁcant overall
heritability ( h2SNP=0.12, P=0.046) and close to signi ﬁcant
genetic correlation with large studies ( rg=0.45, P=0.08),
indicating a meaningful genetic contribution in aggregate.
Subsetting PGC1.5 and UKB by sex showed a high genetic
correlation between women and men for UKB ( rg=1.03, P=
1.6  10−5), but no signi ﬁcant genetic correlation between women
and men in PGC1.5, which was expected, since h2SNPin PGC1.5
men is not signi ﬁcant. Next, focusing on PGC1.5 women only, a
comparison to UKB showed signi ﬁcant genetic correlations with
the overall UKB ( rg=0.46, P=0.0004) and UKB women ( rg=
0.46, P=0.0008), and a slightly lower, but marginally signi ﬁcant
correlation with the smaller UKB male data ( rg=0.44, P=
Overall, these ﬁndings of signi ﬁcant heritability estimates for
PTSD and moderate to high genetic correlations between most
PGC2 subsets, including PGC1.5 to UKB ( rg=0.73, P=0.0005),
are promising for GWAS in these data.GWAS in subjects of European and African ancestry . Our
largest PTSD meta-analysis in subjects of EUA (maximum
number of subjects included in EUA meta-analyses: N=23,212
cases, 151,447 controls, see Table 3for details) identi ﬁed two
independent, genome-wide signi ﬁcant loci ( P<51 0−8), both
mapping to chromosome 6, and sex-strati ﬁed analyses in men
identi ﬁed two additional loci (Fig. 1a, b, respectively). The smaller
meta-analyses in AFA ( N=4363 cases, 10,976 controls) identi-
ﬁed one genome-wide signi ﬁcant locus, and an additional locus
was found in men when strati ﬁed by sex (Fig. 1c, d, respectively).
No genome-wide signi ﬁcant associations were found in meta-
analyses of EUA or AFA women (Supplementary Fig. 3).
Regional plots of the six genomic regions can be found in
Supplementary Figs. 4 –9. The six leading markers show odds
ratios of 1.12 –1.33 and no signi ﬁcant heterogeneity across studies
(Table 3). This is supported by PM-plots (posterior probability
that a SNP effect exists in a given study) showing a high
consistency of effects among the studies predicted to have an
effect13(Supplementary Figs. 10 –15). A z-test on the effect sizes
conﬁrmed similar effects for men and women for the three
leading variants in the joint-sex analyses, and signi ﬁcant sex-
speciﬁcity for the three male hits identi ﬁed in the sex-strati ﬁed
analyses (Supplementary Table 4).
Analyses across ancestry groups . In order to study whether the
genetic associations with PTSD vary across different ancestries,
weﬁrst tried to replicate our six EUA and AFA top hits in the
other main ancestry groups (EUA, AFA and AMA, respectively).
No evidence of replication was found by directly comparing the
six leading markers, nor by investigating the larger genomic
regions harboring the signal (Supplementary Figs. 16 –21). In
addition, we did not identify any genome-wide signi ﬁcant hits by
performing a trans-ethnic genome-wide meta-analysis across the
six main ancestry groups ( N=29,556 cases and 166,145 controls)
under ﬁxed- and random-effect models (Supplementary Fig. 22).
While lack of replication of the 4 EUA hits may not be
conclusive due to limited power in the smaller AFA and AMA
data (Supplementary Tables 5 –6), the 2 hits in AFA provided an
opportunity for a more detailed analysis of ancestry effects.
GWAS in the AFA subjects included standard PCs to control for
average admixture across the genome. However, to precisely infer
local ancestry across the genome of admixed subjects, we further
implemented a local ancestry inference (LAI) pipeline (Supple-
mentary Note 1 and Supplementary Fig. 23). We con ﬁrmed the
AFA top hit rs115539978 to be speci ﬁc to the African ancestral
background (8% MAF on the African, and <1% MAF on theTable 2 Comparison of heritability between European (EUA) and African ancestry (AFA) studies

in the EUA meta-analyses (Supplementary Table 8); however, the
LD-structure in the MHC locus is complex14.
Integration with functional genomic data . Functional mapping
and annotation of the 6 GWAS hits using the FUMA pipeline
conservatively predicted ﬁve genes ZDHHC14 ,PARK2 ,KAZN ,
TMEM51-AS1 and ZNF813 located in EUA risk loci, and ﬁve
distinct genes LINC02335 ,MIR5007 ,TUC338 ,LINC02571 and
HLA-B in AFA risk loci (Table 4). In addition, gene-based ana-
lyses on 18,222 protein-coding genes based on the EUA and AFA
GWAS summary data identi ﬁed two additional gene-wide sig-
niﬁcant loci, represented by SH3RF3 (P=4.28  10−07) and
PODXL (P=2.37  10−06) in the EUA analysis. Gene-based
analyses in AFA did not result in genome-wide signi ﬁcant loci.
The biological function and potential psychiatric relevance of the
12 genes predicted by FUMA are detailed in the Supplementary
Note 1 and discussed below.
We next performed gene-set analyses to understand implicated
genes in the context of pathways and found four signi ﬁcant,
Bonferroni-corrected gene sets (Supplementary Data 2). Of note,
the two gene sets identi ﬁed in the EUA data point towards a role
for the immune system in PTSD. This is supported by a number
of TNF-related genes summarized in a signi ﬁcant gene-set
Annotation of variants in risk loci showed limited evidence of
functionality (Table 4and Supplementary Note 1). Most notably
for the AFA top hit on chromosome 13, when testing for
chromatin interactions using Hi-C data in neural progenitor cells,
signiﬁcant chromatin conformation interactions were observed
between the risk region and a region ~1100 kb upstream
harboring additional non-coding RNAs including LINC00458,
hsa-mir-1297 and LINC00558 as well as a region approximately
820 kb downstream harboring the pseudogene HNF4GP1 (Sup-
plementary Fig. 24). eQTL analyses did not show signi ﬁcant
associations with gene expression. However, the lack of functional
data for this region may be explained by the African ancestry
speciﬁcity of the GWAS ﬁndings since databases available within
the FUMA framework, including GTEx and BrainSpan for eQTL
analyses, are predominantly based on European populations.
Thus, we expanded our analyses for the AFA top hit through
cell culture experiments in lymphoblastoid cell lines (LCLs) from
African subjects (see Methods and Supplementary Note 1). We
show evidence that the African-ancestry-speci ﬁc SNP
rs115539978 seems to capture a genomic region that may
inﬂuence the expression of non-coding RNAs from this PTSD
risk locus in response to increased glucocorticoid receptor
signaling, thus linking this African-speci ﬁc genetic variant to
stress response and non-coding RNA expression (SupplementaryFig. 25).
We further characterized the AFA signal (rs115539978) using
psychophysiology and imaging datasets available through the
Grady Trauma Project (GTPC) and found evidence that this lead
SNP captures a genomic region that is also associated with
increased amygdala volume and fear psychophysiology in a
traumatized population (Supplementary Note 1 and Supplemen-
tary Fig. 26).

powered external study we used the large MVP cohort, including

ﬁrst compared the genetic signals between PGC2 PTSD and MVP
REX EUA and found a highly signi ﬁcant genetic correlation ( rg=
0.80, SE =0.096, P=2.85  10−17). No evidence of replication was

and AFA GWAS for the MVP REX-speci ﬁcs y m p t o m s( S u p p l e -

Polygenic risk scores (PRS) for PTSD . PRS generated from well-
powered GWAS have recently become a tool of high relevance for
polygenic disorders and traits (e.g. ref.15,16). We assessed the
predictive value of PRS for PTSD, using our largest cohort, the
UKB, as a training sample (Fig. 2a). Our analyses were strongest at
a p-value threshold PT=0.3 and showed a highly signi ﬁcant
increase in odds to develop PTSD across PRS quintiles in the
PGC1.5 EUA target sample, with a variance explained on the
liability scale of r2=0.0015 (likelihood ratio test P=5.44  10−7).
Analyses within the UKB show even stronger PRS predictability,
with the highest OR for UKB men with a PRS trained on UKB
women, reaching an OR of 1.39 in the 5th quintile, with an overall
variance explained of r2=0.012 ( P=4.19  10−10).We also tested the overall PGC2 PTSD PRS in the external
MVP replication cohort, using REX as the target for predictions.
PRS predictions were strongest at PT=0.3 and highly signi ﬁcant
(likelihood ratio test P=5.4  10−62, Supplementary Fig. 27).
Mean REX symptoms in MVP EUA participants was 8.48 (4.59
SD), and participants in the 5th quintile of genetic risk had
signiﬁcantly higher REX scores than subjects in the 1st quintile
(beta=0.58, P=1.41  10−48; Fig. 2b).
Genetic correlation of PTSD with other traits and disorders .
Analysis of shared heritability across common disorders of the
brain17and speci ﬁc genetic correlations of psychiatric disorders
with cognitive, anthropomorphic and behavioural measures10,18–20
has been facilitated greatly by the development of a centralized
database of GWAS results including a web interface for LDSC
(LD Hub21). We estimated pairwise genetic correlations ( rg)
between PTSD and 235 disorders/traits and found 21 signi ﬁcant
correlations after conservative Bonferroni correction (Fig. 3,p a n e l
A and Supplementary Data 3). Genetic variation associated with
PTSD was positively correlated with PRS from other psychiatric
traits including depressive symptoms, schizophrenia and neuroti-
cism, as well as epidemiologically comorbid traits such as insom-
nia, smoking behavior, asthma, hip-waist ratio and coronary
artery disease. In contrast, negative rgwith PTSD include subjective

well-being, education, and a strong correlation with parents ’age at
death ( rg=−0.70). Signi ﬁcant positive correlations were also
found for reproductive traits such as the number of children ever
born, and, as previously reported for women22, PTSD was nega-
tively correlated with age at ﬁrst birth.
With the notable exception of asthma, our ﬁndings on PTSD
correspond closely with genetic correlations between these traits
and other psychiatric disorders such as MDD18, SCZ23, BPD19
and ADHD20(Fig. 3, panels B-E). These ﬁndings are not
surprising, as pleiotropic effects (i.e. SNPs impacting multiple
traits) have been widely reported for psychiatric disorders.
In order to test to what degree genome-wide signi ﬁcant
ﬁndings from our GWAS meta-analyses were speci ﬁc to PTSD
rather than driven by correlated traits as identi ﬁed above, we
adjusted the top hits from our analyses for the effects of
genetically correlated psychiatric traits. Since the strongest
correlations were found between PTSD and depressive symptoms
(rg=0.80)and MDD ( rg=0.62), summary statistics from PGC
MDD18, as well as MDD plus BPD and SCZ, were included in the
analyses. Using a recently implemented method applicable to
external GWAS summary data (mtCOJO24) to approximate an
analysis where these traits are regressed out, we found that effect
sizes for the four EUA top hits were not markedly reduced when
adjusted for the effects of MDD, or all three psychiatric traits
tested simultaneously (Supplementary Tables 10, 11). These
ﬁndings indicate that the genetic variants identi ﬁed here are
speciﬁc to PTSD when tested in the context of the three
psychiatric disorders genetically most signi ﬁcantly correlated
PTSD is a common and debilitating condition in ﬂuenced by
genetic factors, yet common genetic risk variants for PTSD have
not been robustly identi ﬁed. The PGC-PTSD combined data from
60 multi-ethnic cohorts (PGC1.5) and the UK Biobank (PGC2)
achieved a sample size of 206,655 participants with 32,428 PTSD
cases, over ten times that of any previous analysis10,25. As has
been demonstrated in GWAS of SCZ23, BPD26, and recently in
MDD18,27and ADHD20, sample size is critical to produce robust
genome-wide signi ﬁcant hits that inform foundational knowledge
on the neurobiology of complex psychiatric conditions. These
results show this is also true of PTSD. This increased power has
led us to draw several major conclusions.
First, our genetic ﬁndings squarely place PTSD among the
other psychiatric disorders in terms of heritability and genetic
relationship with other disorders. While this statement may seem
obvious to some, there remains debate about whether PTSD is
entirely a social construction28. We found substantial SNP-based
heritability (i.e. phenotypic variation explained by genetic dif-
ferences) at 5 –20%, similar to that for major depression18across
methods, studies and ancestries. The heritability results and
pattern of genetic correlations are also consistent with our initial
ﬁndings10and with those from twin studies. PTSD shares com-
mon variant risk with other psychiatric disorders, which show
substantial sharing of common variant risk with one another29.
PTSD was most signi ﬁcantly (genetically) correlated with major
depression, but also with schizophrenia, both of which have
genome-wide signi ﬁcant loci implicated in brain function.
Second, our GWAS analyses identi ﬁed several genetic loci not
previously associated with PTSD. These loci pointed to a number
of different target genes that merit further investigation. With
PARK2 , there is a posited role of dopaminergic systems in PTSD.
The dopaminergic system has a critical role in fear conditioning
which is important in the development and maintenance of

Parkinson Disease and PTSD31–33.PODXL is involved in neural
development and synapse formation34,SH3RF3 is associated with
neurocognition and dementia35,36,ZDHHC14 is associated with
regulation of β-adrenergic receptors37,38, and KAZN is expressed
in the brain39, where it has been found to be underexpressed in
parvalbumin neurons of the superior temporal cortex of schizo-
phrenia cases40and overexpressed in the substantia nigra of
Parkinson ’s cases41. Finally, the HLA-B complex may be related
through the known role of immunity and in ﬂammation in stress-
related disorders42,43. Less is known about the role of the iden-
tiﬁed RNAs LINC02335, MIR5007, TUC338 and LINC02571 in
regards to the biology of PTSD. However, preliminary work from
our group including imaging and psychophysiology highlights the
value of deep phenotyping in conducting functional investiga-
tions into the meaning of GWAS ﬁndings8. Extensive follow-up
work is needed to replicate our ﬁndings and to determine the
function of identi ﬁed genes and their relationship to putative
pathological processes. For example, in SCZ the MHC locus is
now thought to in ﬂuence risk, in part, through pruning of
synapses using immune machinery rather than through classical
immune pathways44. These ancestry-speci ﬁc results are pre-
liminary, and even larger PTSD GWAS will facilitate the identi-
ﬁcation of plausible neurobiological targets for PTSD.
Third, our results also illustrate that there may be genetic
contributions to the well-documented association between PTSD
and dysregulation in in ﬂammatory and immune processes45.I thas been widely recognized that PTSD is associated with a broad
range of adverse physical health conditions over the life course
ranging from type-2-diabetes and cardiovascular disease to
dementia and rheumatoid arthritis46,47. Less is known about the
biological mechanisms driving the relationship between PTSD
and these outcomes. Our genetic correlation analyses may pro-
vide some initial clues for further investigation. For example, we
found a high genetic correlation ( rg=0.49, P=0.0002) between
PTSD and asthma. Our subsequent gene-set and pathway ana-
lyses provide some clues further implicating the immune system.
Of note, these genetic results converge with evidence from epi-
demiologic cohort studies documenting the role of stress-related
disorders such as PTSD in autoimmune diseases48, case-control
studies showing elevations of immune-related biomarkers in
women with PTSD49, and epigenetic studies pointing to the role
of the immune system in PTSD etiology50,51. Further work is
needed to determine whether PTSD has genetic overlap with
immune disorders broadly and the causal direction between
disorders. At minimum, the emerging genetic evidence presented
here suggests that association between PTSD and health condi-
tions may, in some cases, have some genetic origin.
Fourth, PGC-PTSD is distinct in relation to current genomics
consortia due to its high proportion of data from participants of
diverse ancestries. For example, a recent review found that only
three percent of all samples in genetic studies were from African
ancestry52. This contrasts sharply with the 10% of AFA

European ancestryNATURE COMMUNICATIONS | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-019-12576-w ARTICLE
NATURE COMMUNICATIONS | (2019) 10:4558 | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-019-12576-w | www.nature.com/naturecommunications 7
participants in our consortium. We have the ﬁrst heritability
estimates for PTSD in African ancestry: they are similar to EUA,
highly signi ﬁcant in women and lower in men. Our GWAS in
subjects of African ancestry indicated at least one ancestry-
speciﬁc locus using local ancestry methods developed for this
analysis. We note the sample size in the AFA analysis has only
about 15,000 participants, which is small and under-powered,
increasing the chance for false positives. However, other work has
shown that genetic studies of underrepresented populations
afford the opportunity to discover novel loci that are invariant in
European populations53. As others have noted, there are major
limitations in our knowledge of the genetic and environmental
risk architecture of psychiatric disorders in persons of African
descent54. Our ﬁndings provide further evidence of the need to
invest in research that includes diverse ancestral populations, to
expand reference data, and to continue to develop methods to
analyze data from such populations. Until such an investment is
made, we are limited in our ability to understand biological
mechanisms, predict genetic risk55, and produce optimal treat-
ments for non-European populations. African genomes are
characterized by shorter haplotype blocks and contain many
millions more variants per individual than populations outside
Africa56. Further, including data from African populations in
genetic studies of PTSD and other neuropsychiatric disorders
may accelerate genetic discovery and could be useful for ﬁne
mapping disease causing alleles57.
Fifth, although PTSD heritability remained relatively stable
across methods, studies, and ancestries, sex differences in herit-
ability were observed in the overall cohort analyses as well as in
the AFA analyses10. It is of note that the sex differences in her-
itability were not evident in the UK Biobank data, which wehypothesize is due to differences between the PGC1.5 cohorts and
the UK Biobank. PTSD is conditional on trauma exposure, which
is also highly heterogeneous across individuals and populations58.
Unlike PGC1.5, the UK Biobank cohort is comprised of few to no
subjects who experienced military-related trauma. In contrast, a
substantial proportion of men in the PGC1.5 cohorts were from
military cohorts, while virtually all women were civilians. The
environmental experiences (e.g. military versus civilian) and
index traumatic events leading to PTSD in male subjects versus
female subjects could explain observed lower heritability esti-
mates in males in the PGC1.5 cohorts. In future work, we aim to
investigate this empirically by pooling detailed trauma and PTSD
phenotypic information on both males and females and by
modeling the effects of measurement variability on heritability
estimates. Future work could also aim to increase samples of
civilian men and military women to allow for analyses strati ﬁed
by military trauma and sex.
Lastly, we report a signi ﬁcant polygenic risk score for PTSD,
which also signi ﬁcantly predicts re-experiencing symptoms in
independent data from the MVP9. However, larger sample sizes
are needed to achieve sensitivity and speci ﬁcity at levels of clinical
utility16. In the future, polygenic risk may eventually be useful in
algorithms developed to identify vulnerable persons after expo-sure to trauma. PTSD is one of the most theoretically preventable
mental disorders, as many people exposed to trauma come to
clinical attention in ﬁrst response settings such as emergency
rooms, intensive care units, and trauma centers. Controlled
clinical trials show that PTSD risk can be signi ﬁcantly reduced by
early preventive interventions
59,60. However, these interventions
have nontrivial costs, making it infeasible to offer them to all
persons exposed to trauma, given that only a small minority goes
